# تابع

أكيد. سأكمل الآن **الوثائق الثلاثة النهائية المهمة جدًا**: **سجل المخاطر، مصفوفة التتبع، ودليل التشغيل**، لأنها تربط المشروع من المتطلبات حتى الإطلاق والتشغيل اليومي [^82_1][^82_2][^82_3].\
هذه الوثائق هي التي تجعل المشروع “محكَمًا” فعلاً، لأن كل متطلب يصبح له أثر، وكل خطر يصبح له مالك، وكل عملية تشغيل تصبح لها خطوة موثقة [^82_4][^82_5][^82_6].

## 17. سجل المخاطر Risk Register

### 17.1 الغرض

سجل المخاطر هو وثيقة حيّة تُستخدم لاكتشاف المخاطر المحتملة، تقييمها، ترتيبها، وتحديد مسؤول واضح وخطة استجابة لكل خطر [^82_1][^82_7][^82_5].\
في مشروع مثل PrivateMesh / النسر، هذه الوثيقة ليست اختيارية، لأن الأمان والاعتمادية والاتصال اللامركزي كلها مجالات تتغير فيها المخاطر بسرعة [^82_3][^82_5].

### 17.2 قواعد السجل

- يجب أن يكون بسيطًا وواضحًا.
- كل خطر له مالك.
- كل خطر له خطة استجابة.
- يجب مراجعته دوريًا.
- يجب ربطه بالخطة العامة للمشروع [^82_1][^82_7][^82_3].


### 17.3 الحقول القياسية

- **Risk-ID**.
- **اسم الخطر**.
- **الوصف**.
- **المجال**: أمني / تشغيلي / معماري / جدولي.
- **الاحتمال**.
- **الأثر**.
- **الدرجة**.
- **المالك**.
- **خطة الاستجابة**.
- **الحالة**.
- **تاريخ المراجعة**.
- **ملاحظات** [^82_1][^82_5][^82_3].


### 17.4 تصنيف الأثر

- **Critical**: يوقف الإصدار أو يعرّض السرية للخطر.
- **High**: يؤثر بقوة على الأمان أو الاستقرار.
- **Medium**: يسبب مشاكل قابلة للإدارة.
- **Low**: تأثير محدود لكنه موثق [^82_7][^82_3].


### 17.5 أمثلة مخاطر أساسية

- تسرب مفتاح خاص.
- relay خبيث.
- replay attack.
- downgrade attack.
- فساد في التخزين المحلي.
- فشل التوافق بين الإصدارات.
- سوء استخدام AI.
- فقدان سجل التتبع.
- فشل rollback.
- نقص التغطية الاختبارية [^82_8][^82_9][^82_1].


### 17.6 خطة استجابة الخطر

يمكن أن تكون:

- **Mitigate**: تقليل الخطر.
- **Avoid**: إزالة السبب.
- **Transfer**: نقل الأثر لطرف آخر عند الإمكان.
- **Accept**: قبول الخطر إذا كان منخفضًا ومبررًا [^82_5][^82_3].


### 17.7 جدول نموذجي

| Risk-ID | الخطر | الاحتمال | الأثر | المالك | الاستجابة | الحالة |
| :-- | :-- | --: | --: | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| RSK-001 | تسرب مفتاح خاص | High | Critical | Security Lead | Mitigate | Open |
| RSK-002 | relay خبيث | Medium | High | Network Lead | Mitigate | Open |
| RSK-003 | فشل rollback | Medium | High | Release Manager | Mitigate | Planned |
| RSK-004 | downgrade attack | Low | Critical | Crypto Owner | Avoid | Open |

هذا النوع من الجداول هو الأساس العملي لسجل المخاطر [^82_1][^82_7][^82_3].

## 18. مصفوفة التتبع RTM

### 18.1 الغرض

مصفوفة التتبع تربط المتطلبات بحالات الاستخدام والتصميم والاختبارات، وتكشف أي فجوات في التغطية [^82_2][^82_4][^82_6].\
هي الوثيقة التي تمنع وجود متطلبات “معلقة” أو اختبارات “يتيمة” لا ترجع إلى سبب واضح [^82_10][^82_11][^82_6].

### 18.2 القواعد

- كل متطلب له معرف فريد.
- كل متطلب له مصدر.
- كل متطلب له حالة استخدام أو أكثر.
- كل متطلب له تصميم أو مكوّن مرتبط.
- كل متطلب له اختبار واحد على الأقل.
- كل تغيير يجب أن ينعكس في المصفوفة [^82_2][^82_6][^82_12].


### 18.3 الحقول القياسية

- **Req-ID**.
- **المتطلب**.
- **المصدر**.
- **حالة الاستخدام**.
- **العنصر المعماري**.
- **ADR-ID**.
- **Test Case IDs**.
- **الأدلة**.
- **الإصدار**.
- **الحالة** [^82_2][^82_6][^82_12].


### 18.4 جدول نموذجي

| Req-ID | المتطلب | المصدر | Use Case | Design | Test | الحالة |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| R-001 | توليد هوية محلية | رؤية المشروع | UC-001 | Identity Manager | TC-001 | Active |
| R-002 | جلسة آمنة | متطلبات الأمان | UC-002 | Session Manager | TC-010 | Active |
| R-003 | رسالة مشفرة | وثيقة المتطلبات | UC-003 | Message Codec | TC-020 | Active |
| R-004 | Relay آمن | النطاق المرحلي | UC-006 | Relay Adapter | TC-030 | Planned |
| R-005 | Mesh routing | خطة الإصدار 3.0 | UC-008 | Routing Engine | TC-080 | Planned |

الهدف من هذا الجدول هو أن ترى في سطر واحد أين يوجد كل متطلب، وكيف تحقق، ومتى يُعد مكتملًا [^82_4][^82_6][^82_12].

### 18.5 فحص الفجوات

يجب مراجعة RTM دوريًا للتأكد من:

- لا متطلبات بلا اختبار.
- لا اختبارات بلا متطلب.
- لا تصميم بلا متطلب.
- لا متطلب بلا مصدر.
- لا إصدار بلا أثر تتبعي [^82_6][^82_12].


## 19. دليل التشغيل Runbook

### 19.1 الغرض

دليل التشغيل يشرح للمشغل كيف يدير النظام يوميًا وفي الحالات الطارئة [^82_13][^82_14].\
هذا المستند مهم جدًا لأن النظام الآمن لا يكفي أن يكون “مصممًا جيدًا”، بل يجب أن يكون “قابلًا للتشغيل بأمان” [^82_15][^82_13].

### 19.2 الأقسام

- بدء الخدمة.
- إيقاف الخدمة.
- الترقية.
- التراجع rollback.
- استعادة النسخ الاحتياطية.
- تدوير المفاتيح.
- التعامل مع فشل الاتصال.
- التعامل مع ثغرة محتملة.
- مراجعة السجلات.
- التصعيد إلى المسؤول [^82_15][^82_13][^82_14].


### 19.3 قواعد الكتابة

- اكتب الخطوات بصيغة فعلية واضحة.
- اذكر الشروط المسبقة.
- اذكر ما الذي يجب التحقق منه بعد كل خطوة.
- اذكر ما الذي يسبب التراجع.
- اذكر من يملك القرار في الحالات الحرجة [^82_13][^82_14].


### 19.4 نموذج قسم

#### Runbook: تدوير المفاتيح

- **الهدف**: تدوير المفاتيح بعد حادث أو وفق جدول دوري.
- **الشرط المسبق**: وجود نسخة احتياطية مشفرة وموافقة من المسؤول.
- **الخطوات**:

1. إنشاء مفتاح جديد محليًا.
2. تحديث السياسة.
3. إعادة المصادقة مع الأطراف.
4. إبطال المفتاح القديم.
5. التحقق من نجاح الجلسات.
- **التحقق**: تأكد أن الاتصالات القديمة انتهت وأن الاتصالات الجديدة تعمل.
- **العودة**: إذا فشل التحقق، أعد المفتاح السابق مؤقتًا وفق سياسة الطوارئ [^82_16][^82_9][^82_3].


### 19.5 حالات تشغيل مهمة

- تشغيل أولي للإصدار.
- استعادة من نسخة احتياطية.
- إيقاف عقدة مشبوهة.
- عزل جهاز مخترق.
- إصلاح توافق نسخة.
- rollback بعد فشل نشر [^82_15][^82_13][^82_14].


## 20. وثيقة النشر والإصدارات Release Plan

### 20.1 الغرض

تصف كيف ينتقل النظام من إصدار إلى إصدار، وما المعايير التي تمنع أو تسمح بالنشر [^82_17][^82_18][^82_19].\
هذه الوثيقة تربط الخطة التقنية بإدارة المخاطر والنشر الفعلي [^82_15][^82_14].

### 20.2 المحتوى

- رقم الإصدار.
- الهدف.
- الميزات.
- التبعيات.
- المخاطر.
- الاختبارات المطلوبة.
- خطة rollback.
- خطة المراقبة.
- المخرجات المتوقعة [^82_13][^82_14][^82_3].


### 20.3 سياسة النشر

- **Alpha**: داخلي فقط.
- **Beta**: محدود.
- **Staged**: تدريجي.
- **General**: بعد اجتياز gates.
- **Hotfix**: للحالات الحرجة فقط [^82_17][^82_20][^82_13].


### 20.4 مثال

- **1.0**: النواة الآمنة.
- **1.1**: تحسين الاستقرار.
- **2.0**: discovery وrelay.
- **2.1**: الملفات وتعدد الأجهزة.
- **3.0**: mesh.
- **3.1**: مراقبة وحوكمة [^82_21][^82_18][^82_19].


## 21. قائمة التحقق قبل الإطلاق Release Checklist

### 21.1 العناصر

- اكتمال الوثائق الأساسية.
- اكتمال RTM.
- اكتمال ADRs.
- لا توجد ثغرات حرجة مفتوحة.
- نجاح الاختبارات.
- جاهزية rollback.
- جاهزية المراقبة.
- موافقة المالك [^82_15][^82_13][^82_3].


### 21.2 الاستخدام

هذه القائمة تُستخدم قبل أي إصدار ولا تُختصر؛ لأنها تمنع الإطلاق غير المنضبط [^82_13][^82_14].

## 22. خطة المراجعة الدورية

### 22.1 ماذا يُراجع؟

- وثيقة المخاطر.
- RTM.
- ADR.
- المتطلبات.
- قائمة الإطلاق.
- runbooks [^82_11][^82_1][^82_6].


### 22.2 متى؟

- أسبوعيًا للمخاطر.
- عند كل إصدار للمصفوفة والقرارات.
- عند كل تغيير كبير للوثائق المتأثرة [^82_1][^82_3].


## 23. الهيكل النهائي الجاهز للمستودع

```text
docs/
  00-charter.md
  01-vision-scope.md
  02-requirements.md
  03-use-cases.md
  04-architecture.md
  05-threat-model.md
  06-security-key-management.md
  07-protocol-spec.md
  08-discovery-relay.md
  09-mesh-routing.md
  10-ai-layer.md
  11-release-plan.md
  12-testing-quality.md
  13-operations-runbook.md
  14-governance-change.md
  15-adr/
  16-traceability-matrix.md
  17-risk-register.md
  18-release-checklist.md
  appendices/
    glossary.md
    abbreviations.md
    failure-scenarios.md
    operational-contacts.md
  llms.txt
```

هذا الترتيب يعكس أفضل الممارسات في التوثيق والمراجعة والربط بين عناصر المشروع [^82_22][^82_11][^82_6].

إذا أردت، أستطيع الآن أن أقدم لك **النص الكامل النهائي بصيغة توثيقية جاهزة** لكل واحد من هذه الملفات، أو أبدأ مباشرة بإنشاء **ملف llms.txt** و**مصفوفة التتبع** و**سجل المخاطر** بصيغة عملية قابلة للنسخ.

# ابدأ بإنشاء ملف llms.txt ومصفوفة التتبع وسجل المخاطر أولاً

أستطيع البدء بهذه الملفات فورًا، لكن بيئة التنفيذ الحالية لا تسمح بإنشاء الملفات عبر أداة الكود هنا. لذلك أضعها لك الآن بصيغة جاهزة للنسخ مباشرة إلى الملفات المطلوبة: `llms.txt` و`traceability_matrix.md` و`risk_register.md` [^83_1][^83_2][^83_3].

## llms.txt

```text
# PrivateMesh / النسر

> Secure decentralized communication system focused on identity, encrypted messaging, relay transport, and phased mesh networking.

## Start Here
- [00-charter.md](docs/00-charter.md): Project purpose, goals, stakeholders, and boundaries.
- [01-vision-scope.md](docs/01-vision-scope.md): Vision, scope, principles, out-of-scope items, and release stages.
- [02-requirements.md](docs/02-requirements.md): Functional and non-functional requirements with priorities.

## Core Design
- [03-use-cases.md](docs/03-use-cases.md): Primary user and system scenarios.
- [04-architecture.md](docs/04-architecture.md): System layers, components, trust boundaries, and design decisions.
- [05-threat-model.md](docs/05-threat-model.md): Assets, attackers, threats, and mitigations.
- [06-security-key-management.md](docs/06-security-key-management.md): Key generation, storage, rotation, revocation, and recovery.
- [07-protocol-spec.md](docs/07-protocol-spec.md): Message format, session states, validation rules, and versioning.

## Networking
- [08-discovery-relay.md](docs/08-discovery-relay.md): Discovery, bootstrap, relay selection, retry, and fallback.
- [09-mesh-routing.md](docs/09-mesh-routing.md): Multi-hop routing, resilience, route repair, and loop prevention.

## AI Layer
- [10-ai-layer.md](docs/10-ai-layer.md): AI-assisted features, permissions, privacy constraints, and safety limits.

## Delivery
- [11-release-plan.md](docs/11-release-plan.md): Version roadmap, release gates, rollout strategy, and rollback plan.
- [12-testing-quality.md](docs/12-testing-quality.md): Test strategy, quality gates, and acceptance criteria.
- [13-operations-runbook.md](docs/13-operations-runbook.md): Deployment, monitoring, restore, incident handling, and maintenance.
- [14-governance-change.md](docs/14-governance-change.md): Roles, approvals, change control, and documentation governance.

## Technical Appendices
- [16-traceability-matrix.md](docs/16-traceability-matrix.md): Requirement-to-use-case-to-design-to-test mapping.
- [17-risk-register.md](docs/17-risk-register.md): Project risks, owners, treatments, and review status.
- [18-release-checklist.md](docs/18-release-checklist.md): Pre-release verification checklist.
- [appendices/glossary.md](docs/appendices/glossary.md): Key terms and definitions.
- [appendices/abbreviations.md](docs/appendices/abbreviations.md): Acronyms and shorthand.
- [appendices/failure-scenarios.md](docs/appendices/failure-scenarios.md): Expected failure and attack scenarios.
- [appendices/operational-contacts.md](docs/appendices/operational-contacts.md): Owners and escalation contacts.
```

```markdown
| Req-ID | Requirement | Source | Use Case | Design Component | ADR-ID | Test Case IDs | Evidence | Release | Status |

| R-003 | Encrypt and authenticate messages end-to-end | 02-requirements / 05-threat-model | UC-003, UC-004 | Message Codec, Crypto Engine | ADR-0003 | TC-020, TC-021 | Pending | 1.0 | Active |
| R-004 | Store local data encrypted at rest | 02-requirements | UC-001, UC-004 | Local Secure Store | ADR-0004 | TC-030 | Pending | 1.0 | Active |
| R-005 | Discover nearby peers within allowed policy | 01-vision-scope / 08-discovery-relay | UC-005 | Discovery Service | ADR-0005 | TC-040 | Pending | 2.0 | Planned |

| R-007 | Resume communication after interruption | 02-requirements | UC-002, UC-004 | Session Manager | ADR-0007 | TC-012, TC-013 | Pending | 2.0 | Planned |
| R-008 | Transfer files securely in chunks | 02-requirements / 07-protocol-spec | UC-007 | File Transfer Module | ADR-0008 | TC-050, TC-051 | Pending | 2.1 | Planned |
| R-009 | Support the same account on multiple devices | 01-vision-scope / 02-requirements | UC-001, UC-007 | Device Sync Manager | ADR-0009 | TC-060 | Pending | 2.1 | Planned |
| R-010 | Route traffic through multi-hop mesh paths | 09-mesh-routing | UC-008 | Routing Engine | ADR-0010 | TC-080, TC-081 | Pending | 3.0 | Planned |
| R-011 | Detect and report suspicious network behavior | 10-ai-layer / 05-threat-model | UC-006, UC-008 | AI Assistant Layer | ADR-0011 | TC-090 | Pending | 3.1+ | Planned |
```

## risk_register.md

```markdown
# Risk Register

| Risk-ID | Risk | Category | Likelihood | Impact | Score | Owner | Response | Status | Last Review |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| RSK-001 | Private key leakage from device compromise | Security | High | Critical | 25 | Security Lead | Mitigate: hardware-backed storage where possible, encrypted backup policy, revocation procedure | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-002 | Relay node attempts traffic analysis or abuse | Security / Network | Medium | High | 16 | Network Lead | Mitigate: relay minimization, content encryption, health checks, reputation signals | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-003 | Replay attack against message or handshake flow | Security | Medium | Critical | 20 | Crypto Owner | Mitigate: nonce, sequence checks, session binding, expiry rules | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-004 | Downgrade to weaker protocol or legacy mode | Security / Architecture | Low | Critical | 15 | Architecture Lead | Avoid: explicit version negotiation, reject unsafe fallback | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-005 | Local storage corruption causes message loss | Reliability | Medium | High | 16 | Storage Owner | Mitigate: integrity checks, journaling, recovery path | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-006 | Incomplete traceability across requirements and tests | Process | Medium | High | 16 | Documentation Owner | Mitigate: maintain RTM, review on each release gate | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-007 | Release failure with no safe rollback path | Operations | Medium | High | 16 | Release Manager | Mitigate: staged rollout, rollback checklist, pre-release validation | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-008 | AI layer exposes sensitive metadata or content | AI / Privacy | Medium | Critical | 20 | AI Safety Reviewer | Mitigate: redaction, local processing, permission boundaries, audit logs | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-009 | Mesh routing complexity delays core delivery | Schedule / Architecture | Medium | High | 16 | Project Owner | Mitigate: defer mesh until V2.x passes gates, keep V1 minimal | Open | 2026-09-28 |
| RSK-010 | Insufficient test coverage for security-critical paths | Quality | High | High | 20 | QA Lead | Mitigate: mandatory security tests, fuzzing, regression suite | Open | 2026-09-28 |
```

# تابع

تمام. سأكمل الآن بثلاث وثائق عملية جدًا: **قائمة التحقق قبل الإطلاق، دليل التشغيل، وملف ADR الأول**، لأن هذه هي القطعة التي تربط التصميم بالتنفيذ الفعلي وتشغل المشروع بأمان [^84_1][^84_2][^84_3].\
سأكتبها بصيغة جاهزة للنسخ إلى الملفات، مع الالتزام بأن كل ADR يصف قرارًا واحدًا فقط وبأسلوب واضح ومختصر [^84_1][^84_4][^84_5].

## 18. release-checklist.md

```markdown
# Release Checklist

## Purpose
This checklist must be completed before any release is approved.

## Pre-Release Checks
- [ ] All required documents are updated.
- [ ] Requirements traceability matrix is current.
- [ ] Risk register is reviewed.
- [ ] No critical security issues remain open.
- [ ] All required ADRs are approved or superseded.
- [ ] Test suite passed for the target release scope.
- [ ] Security tests passed.
- [ ] Regression tests passed.
- [ ] Performance checks are within limits.
- [ ] Rollback plan is documented and verified.
- [ ] Backup and restore procedures are validated.
- [ ] Release notes are written.
- [ ] Known issues are documented.
- [ ] Monitoring and alerting are ready.
- [ ] Operational contacts are confirmed.
- [ ] Sign-off has been obtained from the release owner.

## Go/No-Go Criteria
### Go
- No unresolved critical defects.
- No unresolved critical security findings.
- Rollback is tested and available.
- Required approvals are completed.

### No-Go
- Critical defect exists.
- Security issue is unresolved.
- Required evidence is missing.
- Rollback is untested.

## Post-Release Checks
- [ ] Release health is stable.
- [ ] Errors are within expected thresholds.
- [ ] Logs and telemetry are accessible.
- [ ] Rollback remains available if needed.
- [ ] Release notes have been archived.
```


## 13. operations-runbook.md

```markdown
# Operations Runbook

## Purpose
This runbook defines the procedures for deploying, operating, monitoring, and recovering the system safely.

## 1. Start Service
### Preconditions
- Approved release is available.
- Environment is prepared.
- Required secrets are present.

### Steps
1. Verify configuration.
2. Verify dependencies.
3. Start the service.
4. Confirm health checks pass.
5. Confirm network connectivity.
6. Confirm log output is normal.

### Exit Criteria
- Service is healthy.
- No critical errors are present.

## 2. Stop Service
### Steps
1. Notify stakeholders if required.
2. Stop incoming traffic.
3. Flush pending operations if safe.
4. Stop the service.
5. Confirm shutdown completed.

## 3. Deploy Update
### Steps
1. Confirm release checklist is complete.
2. Back up current state.
3. Deploy the new version.
4. Run smoke tests.
5. Verify monitoring.
6. Keep rollback option available.

## 4. Roll Back
### Trigger Conditions
- Critical failure after deployment.
- Security issue detected.
- Data integrity issue.
- Major service instability.

### Steps
1. Stop new traffic.
2. Restore previous release.
3. Restore previous configuration if needed.
4. Validate service health.
5. Confirm users are reconnected if applicable.

## 5. Restore From Backup
### Steps
1. Identify restore point.
2. Validate backup integrity.
3. Restore data.
4. Validate integrity after restore.
5. Resume service.

## 6. Key Rotation
### Steps
1. Generate new key pair locally.
2. Update trust mappings.
3. Reauthenticate peers.
4. Revoke old key.
5. Confirm sessions recover or restart safely.

## 7. Incident Handling
### Examples
- Suspicious relay behavior.
- Possible key compromise.
- Message integrity failure.
- Repeated authentication failure.

### Steps
1. Contain the issue.
2. Preserve evidence.
3. Notify the owner.
4. Apply mitigation.
5. Document the incident.
6. Review follow-up actions.

## 8. Monitoring
### Checkpoints
- Service health.
- Message delivery success.
- Authentication failures.
- Relay anomalies.
- Storage errors.
- Resource usage.

## 9. Logging
- Use structured logs where possible.
- Avoid sensitive content in logs.
- Retain logs according to policy.
- Protect logs from unauthorized access.

## 10. Contacts
- Release Owner
- Security Lead
- Architecture Lead
- Operations Lead
- Documentation Owner
```


## 15. docs/adr/ADR-0001.md

```markdown
# ADR-0001: Generate device identity locally

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
The system requires a trustworthy device identity for secure messaging, session establishment, and peer recognition.  
A central identity authority would increase coupling and weaken the decentralized design goal. The project also requires that private keys remain under local control.

## Decision
We will generate each device identity locally on the device and store the private key only in protected local storage.

## Alternatives Considered
1. Central identity server.
2. Cloud-managed identity service.
3. Locally generated identity.

## Decision Rationale
- Local generation preserves decentralization.
- The private key stays under device control.
- It reduces the trust required in external infrastructure.
- It fits the project’s privacy-first model.

## Consequences
### Positive
- Better privacy.
- Less dependency on central services.
- Simpler threat boundary for identity generation.

### Negative
- Device loss can be more serious.
- Recovery and revocation must be designed carefully.
- Backup and rotation procedures become more important.

## Notes
This ADR applies to the initial version of the system and may be refined by later ADRs if recovery or multi-device identity handling changes.
```


## 15. docs/adr/ADR-0002.md

```markdown
# ADR-0002: Use authenticated session keys for messaging

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
Messages must be encrypted and protected against replay, tampering, and unauthorized access. Long-lived static keys should not be used directly for every message.

## Decision
We will establish authenticated session keys during the handshake and use them for message encryption and integrity protection.

## Alternatives Considered
1. Encrypt every message directly with the long-term identity key.
2. Use unauthenticated session encryption.
3. Use authenticated session keys.

## Decision Rationale
- Session keys reduce exposure of long-term identity material.
- Authenticated encryption supports confidentiality and integrity.
- It allows key rotation and session renewal.

## Consequences
### Positive
- Better security posture.
- Cleaner separation between identity and transport security.
- Easier revocation and session renewal.

### Negative
- More complex handshake logic.
- More state to manage during session lifetime.
```


## 15. docs/adr/ADR-0003.md

```markdown
# ADR-0003: Reject protocol downgrade by default

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
Attackers may try to force peers to fall back to weaker protocol behavior. This could reduce security and break the project’s assurance model.

## Decision
We will reject protocol downgrade attempts by default and only allow explicitly approved compatibility paths.

## Alternatives Considered
1. Silent fallback to older protocol versions.
2. Manual fallback only when explicitly approved.
3. Hard reject downgrade attempts by default.

## Decision Rationale
- Prevents security regression.
- Reduces ambiguity in peer negotiation.
- Makes protocol behavior easier to audit.

## Consequences
### Positive
- Stronger security.
- Clear version handling.
- Better protection against downgrade attacks.

### Negative
- Some legacy peers may fail to connect.
- Compatibility must be managed intentionally.
```


## 15. docs/adr/ADR-0004.md

```markdown
# ADR-0004: Store local private keys in protected local storage

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
Private keys are the most sensitive local asset. They must be protected at rest and unavailable in plaintext to ordinary application processes.

## Decision
We will store private keys only in protected local storage and never expose them in plaintext form in logs, files, or protocol payloads.

## Alternatives Considered
1. Plaintext storage.
2. Encrypted file storage without access control.
3. Protected local storage with encryption and access control.

## Decision Rationale
- Minimizes key exposure.
- Supports device-local trust.
- Aligns with the security model.

## Consequences
### Positive
- Reduced risk of immediate compromise.
- Easier auditing of sensitive handling.

### Negative
- Recovery becomes more constrained.
- Backup design must be careful.
```


## 15. docs/adr/ADR-0005.md

```markdown
# ADR-0005: Defer mesh routing until core secure messaging is stable

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
Mesh routing adds significant complexity, routing risk, and testing burden. The system must first prove that identity, handshake, messaging, and relay behavior are stable.

## Decision
We will defer mesh routing to a later release and keep initial releases focused on secure core messaging and relay-based transport.

## Alternatives Considered
1. Build mesh routing in the first release.
2. Build a limited mesh in parallel.
3. Defer mesh routing until core messaging is stable.

## Decision Rationale
- Reduces early complexity.
- Keeps the project testable.
- Focuses effort on the highest-risk security foundations first.

## Consequences
### Positive
- Faster delivery of a secure core.
- Lower risk of architectural churn.
- Better test coverage of foundational features.

### Negative
- Mesh capability arrives later.
- Some advanced use cases are delayed.
```

ضع هذه الملفات بهذا الشكل:

```text
    ADR-0002.md
    ADR-0003.md
    ADR-0004.md
    ADR-0005.md
```


## الخطوة التالية

الآن أصبحت عندك:

- `llms.txt`
- `traceability_matrix.md`
- `risk_register.md`
- `release-checklist.md`
- `operations-runbook.md`
- أول مجموعة ADRs

الخطوة التالية المنطقية هي أن أكتب لك:

1. **وثيقة المعمارية الكاملة**
2. **وثيقة التهديدات الكاملة**
3. **وثيقة البروتوكول الكاملة**
4. **وثيقة الأمان وإدارة المفاتيح الكاملة**

هل أبدأ مباشرة بــ **وثيقة المعمارية الكاملة**؟

[^84_1]: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/well-architected/architect-role/architecture-decision-record

[^84_2]: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/architectural-decision-records/adr-process.html

[^84_3]: https://www.archyl.com/blog/architecture-decision-records-complete-guide

[^84_4]: https://github.com/architecture-decision-record/architecture-decision-record

[^84_6]: https://docs.cloud.google.com/architecture/architecture-decision-records

# تابع تغطيه الكل كامل مكتمل

أكيد. سأكمل الآن **الجزء الأخير من الحزمة كاملة ومكتملة**: **وثيقة المعمارية، وثيقة التهديدات، وثيقة الأمان وإدارة المفاتيح، وثيقة البروتوكول، ووثيقة الحوكمة النهائية**، بحيث يصبح عندك هيكل مشروع متكامل من الفهرس حتى التشغيل [^85_1][^85_2][^85_3].\
سأكتبها الآن بصيغة رسمية جاهزة للنسخ، مع جعل المعمارية مرتبطة بحالات الاستخدام والقرارات المعمارية، والأمن مرتبطًا بإدارة دورة حياة المفاتيح، والعمليات مرتبطة بدليل تشغيل فعلي [^85_1][^85_2][^85_4].

## 04. وثيقة المعمارية التقنية

### 4.1 الهدف

توضح هذه الوثيقة البنية الداخلية للنظام، وتربط بين الرؤية والمتطلبات وحالات الاستخدام والتنفيذ [^85_1][^85_2].\
ينبغي أن تُبنى حول سيناريوهات واقعية مثل إنشاء الهوية، المصافحة، إرسال الرسائل، استعادة الجلسات، الاكتشاف، والـ relay، لا حول مخطط نظري مجرد [^85_1][^85_2].

### 4.2 النظرة العامة

النظام يتكون من طبقات رئيسية:

- **Identity Layer**.
- **Session Layer**.
- **Message Layer**.
- **Transport Layer**.
- **Relay Layer**.
- **Mesh Routing Layer**.
- **Storage Layer**.
- **AI Assistance Layer** [^85_1][^85_2].


### 4.3 سياق النظام

يعمل النظام بين:

- أجهزة المستخدمين.
- عقد relay.
- عقد mesh مستقبلية.
- بيئة تخزين محلية مشفرة.
- طبقة AI مساعدة محدودة الصلاحيات [^85_1][^85_2].


### 4.4 المكونات الأساسية

- **Identity Manager**: إنشاء وإدارة الهوية.
- **Key Manager**: إدارة المفاتيح.
- **Session Manager**: إنشاء الجلسات وتجديدها.
- **Message Codec**: ترميز الرسائل وتشفيرها.
- **Discovery Service**: اكتشاف الأجهزة.
- **Relay Adapter**: تمرير الرسائل.
- **Routing Engine**: التوجيه المتعدد.
- **Secure Store**: التخزين المحلي.
- **AI Assistant**: وظائف غير حساسة [^85_1][^85_2].


### 4.5 خصائص الجودة

- **الخصوصية**: تقليل metadata.
- **الأمان**: تشفير ومصادقة وتحقق.
- **الاعتمادية**: تحمل الفشل والاستعادة.
- **الأداء**: تقليل التأخير.
- **القابلية للتوسع**: إضافة أجهزة وعقد دون كسر النواة.
- **قابلية الصيانة**: فصل واضح للمسؤوليات [^85_1][^85_2].


### 4.6 الأمن المعماري

- لا اعتماد معماري على مركز ثقة واحد.
- الفصل بين المحتوى ومسار النقل.
- التشفير في النقل والتخزين.
- التحقق من سلامة البيانات.
- رفض التراجع غير الآمن.
- تسجيل كل قرار معماري في ADR [^85_5][^85_6][^85_2].


### 4.7 النشر والتشغيل

- بيئات منفصلة: dev / staging / production.
- CI/CD موثق.
- مراقبة وسجلات.
- خطة rollback.
- runbooks تشغيلية لكل إجراء حساس [^85_4][^85_2][^85_7].


### 4.8 ADRs

كل قرار مهم يجب أن يتوفر له ADR يشمل:

- السياق.
- القرار.
- البدائل.
- العواقب.
- الحالة [^85_5][^85_6][^85_8].


## 05. وثيقة نموذج التهديد

### 5.1 الهدف

تحدد هذه الوثيقة الأصول، المهاجمين، التهديدات، والضوابط المطلوبة لتقليل المخاطر [^85_9][^85_10][^85_3].\
يجب تحديثها مع كل تغيير معماري أو بروتوكولي أو أمني [^85_9][^85_3].

### 5.2 الأصول

- المفاتيح الخاصة.
- الرسائل.
- الجلسات.
- metadata.
- ملفات المستخدم.
- سجلات النظام.
- مسارات التحديث [^85_10][^85_3].


### 5.3 المهاجمون

- مهاجم شبكة.
- مهاجم relay.
- مهاجم جهاز.
- مهاجم داخلي.
- مهاجم يحاول downgrade.
- مهاجم replay.
- مهاجم يستغل AI أو التسريب [^85_9][^85_3].


### 5.4 التهديدات

- التنصت.
- انتحال الهوية.
- replay.
- tampering.
- key theft.
- downgrade.
- traffic analysis.
- malicious relay.
- data loss.
- log leakage [^85_10][^85_3].


### 5.5 الضوابط

- تشفير end-to-end.
- مصادقة متبادلة.
- nonces وsequence numbers.
- session binding.
- حماية التخزين المحلي.
- تشفير النسخ الاحتياطية.
- التحكم في الوصول.
- audit trail.
- rate limiting.
- explicit protocol versioning [^85_3][^85_11].


### 5.6 المخاطر المتبقية

لا يمكن إزالة كل المخاطر بالكامل، لذلك يجب توثيق المخاطر المتبقية واعتمادها أو رفضها بشكل صريح [^85_9][^85_10][^85_3].

## 06. وثيقة الأمان وإدارة المفاتيح

### 6.1 الهدف

تحدد هذه الوثيقة دورة حياة المفاتيح من الإنشاء حتى الإتلاف، وكيف تُحمى أثناء التخزين والاستعمال والاستعادة [^85_10][^85_3][^85_11].

### 6.2 المبادئ

- المفاتيح تُنشأ محليًا.
- المفتاح الخاص لا يُكشف كنص صريح.
- كل عملية على المفاتيح يجب أن تكون مبررة ومراقبة.
- النسخ الاحتياطي يجب أن يكون مشفرًا ومقيدًا.
- الإتلاف يجب أن يكون آمنًا وموثقًا [^85_3][^85_11][^85_12].


### 6.3 دورة الحياة

1. **Generation**.
2. **Registration**.
3. **Storage**.
4. **Use**.
5. **Rotation**.
6. **Revocation**.
7. **Backup**.
8. **Recovery**.
9. **Destruction** [^85_3][^85_11].

### 6.4 الضوابط التشغيلية

- تشفير المفاتيح في التخزين.
- حماية الوصول.
- تدوير دوري أو استثنائي عند الاشتباه.
- منع التصدير غير المصرح.
- مراجعة السجل عند أي تغير مفاتيح [^85_3][^85_11][^85_12].


### 6.5 الأدوار

- **Key Owner**.
- **Security Reviewer**.
- **Operations Owner**.
- **Recovery Approver** [^85_3][^85_11].


## 07. وثيقة مواصفات البروتوكول

### 7.1 الهدف

تعريف الرسائل، حالات الجلسة، وآلية التفاوض والتحقق والرفض [^85_1][^85_2].

### 7.2 بنية الرسالة

- Version.
- Type.
- Sender ID.
- Receiver ID.
- Session ID.
- Timestamp.
- Nonce.
- Payload.
- Signature.
- Integrity field [^85_1][^85_2].


### 7.3 حالات البروتوكول

- Init.
- Hello.
- Challenge.
- Verified.
- Encrypted.
- Ack.
- Retry.
- Error.
- Revoked.
- Rekey [^85_1][^85_2].


### 7.4 القواعد

- رفض downgrade.
- رفض الرسائل غير الموقعة حيث يلزم.
- منع replay.
- التحقق من حدود البيانات.
- التأكد من توافق الإصدار.
- توثيق أي استثناء صراحة [^85_5][^85_6][^85_2].


### 7.5 التوافق

أي تغيير في البروتوكول يجب أن يُرافقه:

- ADR.
- تحديث للمواصفات.
- تعديل RTM.
- اختبار رجوعي.
- ملاحظات إصدار [^85_6][^85_13][^85_2].


## 08. وثيقة الاكتشاف والترحيل

### 8.1 الهدف

تصف هذه الوثيقة كيف تتعرف الأجهزة على بعضها وكيف تمر الرسائل عند غياب اتصال مباشر [^85_4][^85_14][^85_7].

### 8.2 العناصر

- Discovery.
- Bootstrap.
- Relay selection.
- Retry policy.
- Fallback.
- Local-only mode.
- Health checks [^85_4][^85_14].


### 8.3 السياسات

- relay لا يرى المحتوى.
- الاكتشاف يكون ضمن السياسة المسموح بها.
- أي retry يجب أن يكون محددًا بحدود.
- الترحيل لا يجب أن يكسر الخصوصية.
- كل فشل يجب أن يسجل [^85_4][^85_14].


## 09. وثيقة mesh والتوجيه

### 9.1 الهدف

التوسع من relay بسيط إلى توجيه متعدد القفزات بطريقة آمنة ومضبوطة [^85_1][^85_2].

### 9.2 مبادئ التوجيه

- منع الحلقات.
- مسارات قصيرة قدر الإمكان.
- تقييم موثوقية العقد.
- إعادة إصلاح المسار عند الفشل.
- عدم كشف المحتوى للعقد الوسيطة [^85_1][^85_2].


### 9.3 الاعتمادية

- fallback عند فشل المسار.
- إعادة بناء التوجيه عند تغير الشبكة.
- مراقبة استقرار العقد [^85_4][^85_14].


## 10. وثيقة الذكاء المساعد

### 10.1 الهدف

تحديد دور AI داخل النظام بوضوح صارم [^85_7][^85_1].

### 10.2 المسموح

- تلخيص.
- تصنيف.
- اقتراح.
- رصد أنماط غير طبيعية.
- مساعدة التوثيق [^85_7].


### 10.3 الممنوع

- إدارة المفاتيح.
- اتخاذ قرار أمني نهائي.
- الوصول إلى محتوى حساس بلا قيود.
- تصدير بيانات حساسة خارج الحدود [^85_1][^85_3].


### 10.4 الضوابط

- local-first حيث أمكن.
- redaction.
- permissions.
- audit logs.
- explicit opt-in [^85_1][^85_7].


## 11. خطة الإصدارات

### 11.1 0.1

إثبات الفكرة والنواة.

### 11.2 1.0

النواة الآمنة.

### 11.3 1.1

استقرار وتحسين.

### 11.4 2.0

Discovery + Relay.

### 11.5 2.1

Files + Multi-device.

### 11.6 3.0

Mesh routing.

### 11.7 3.1+

Optimization + governance + AI hardening [^85_1][^85_2].

## 12. الاختبارات والجودة

### 12.1 أنواع الاختبار

- Unit.
- Integration.
- Security.
- Fuzzing.
- Performance.
- Compatibility.
- Acceptance [^85_1][^85_2].


### 12.2 معايير القبول

- لا ثغرات حرجة مفتوحة.
- البروتوكول يعمل كما هو موثق.
- rollback متاح ومختبر.
- التقارير والسجلات محدثة [^85_4][^85_7].


## 13. النشر والتشغيل

### 13.1 التشغيل

- Deployment.
- Monitoring.
- Alerts.
- Logs.
- Backup.
- Restore.
- Incident response [^85_4][^85_7].


### 13.2 runbooks

كل إجراء متكرر أو حساس يجب أن يكون له runbook واضح قابل للتنفيذ [^85_4][^85_14].

## 14. الحوكمة وإدارة التغيير

### 14.1 الأدوار

- Owner.
- Reviewer.
- Approver.
- Security reviewer.
- Operations reviewer [^85_5][^85_6][^85_15].


### 14.2 قواعد التغيير

- كل تغيير مؤثر = ADR.
- كل إصدار = checklist.
- كل مخاطر = risk register.
- كل متطلب = traceability [^85_5][^85_13][^85_16].

# نعم

أكيد. أبدأ الآن بكتابة **ملف 00-charter.md** ثم **01-vision-scope.md** ثم **02-requirements.md** بصيغة رسمية كاملة، مع جعل كل متطلب قابلًا للتتبع والاختبار ويُبنى عليه RTM لاحقًا [^86_1][^86_2][^86_3].\
سأحافظ على نفس البنية التي اتفقنا عليها: ميثاق المشروع أولًا، ثم الرؤية والنطاق، ثم المتطلبات، لأن هذا هو التسلسل الذي يضمن وضوح المصدر والهدف والقياس [^86_4][^86_5][^86_6].

## 00-charter.md

```markdown
# Project Charter
## Project Name
PrivateMesh / النسر

## Document Purpose
This document formally authorizes the project, defines its purpose, sets expectations, and identifies the people responsible for delivery and governance.

## Problem Statement
Current communication systems often rely on centralized trust assumptions or expose metadata and message content in ways that weaken privacy, resilience, and user control. This project addresses that gap by building a decentralized, secure, phased communication system.

## Project Purpose
The purpose of this project is to design and implement a privacy-first communication platform that supports secure identity, encrypted messaging, relay transport, and later mesh networking.

## Project Objectives
- Create a secure device identity model.
- Establish authenticated secure sessions.
- Protect message confidentiality and integrity.
- Store local data securely.
- Support discovery and relay in later releases.
- Introduce mesh networking only after the secure core is stable.
- Maintain full documentation, traceability, and governance.

## Success Criteria
- The secure core works reliably in version 1.0.
- Requirements are traceable to use cases, architecture, tests, and releases.
- Security-critical decisions are documented in ADRs.
- Release gates prevent unsafe promotion of unstable versions.

## High-Level Scope
### In Scope
- Device identity.
- Secure handshake.
- Session management.
- Encrypted messaging.
- Secure local storage.
- Discovery and relay.
- File transfer.
- Multi-device support.
- Mesh routing in later releases.
- Documentation, testing, release planning, and operations.

### Out of Scope
- Centralized trust authority.
- Public social networking features.
- Uncontrolled AI access to sensitive data.
- Mesh routing before the secure core is validated.
- Security decisions made by AI without human approval.

## Key Stakeholders
- Project Owner
- Technical Lead
- Security Lead
- Operations Lead
- Documentation Owner
- QA Lead
- AI Safety Reviewer

## Assumptions
- Users want secure peer-to-peer communication.
- Devices can perform local key generation.
- The system may run across multiple releases.
- Documentation will be maintained continuously.

## Constraints
- Security must take priority over convenience.
- Private keys must remain under local control.
- The design must allow staged rollout.
- The project must remain testable and auditable.

## Governance
All major technical decisions must be documented. Any change that affects architecture, security, protocol behavior, or release behavior must be recorded and reviewed before implementation.

## Approval
This charter becomes effective once approved by the project owner and the technical lead.
```


## 01-vision-scope.md

```markdown
# Vision and Scope

## Vision
Build a secure, decentralized, privacy-first communication system that begins with a strong encrypted core and grows step by step into a resilient mesh-capable platform.

## Mission
Provide users with secure identity, authenticated sessions, encrypted messaging, and controlled networking without relying on a central trust anchor.

## Product Principles
- Privacy first.
- Security first.
- Local key control.
- Minimal metadata.
- Clear documentation.
- Incremental delivery.
- Testability at every stage.

## Product Scope
### Core Scope
- Device identity generation.
- Secure handshake.
- Session establishment and renewal.
- Encrypted message exchange.
- Secure local storage.
- Integrity verification.
- Release governance.
- Testing and operations support.

### Extended Scope
- Discovery of peers.
- Relay-based delivery.
- File transfer.
- Multi-device support.
- Mesh routing.
- Operational tooling.
- AI-assisted documentation and anomaly support.

## Out of Scope
- Centralized identity authority.
- Broad consumer social features.
- Unrestricted AI access to sensitive data.
- Advanced mesh routing before the secure core stabilizes.
- Features that weaken security in exchange for convenience.

## Release Stages
### Version 0.1
Proof of concept focused on identity and basic secure structure.

### Version 1.0
Secure core: identity, handshake, sessions, encrypted messaging, local storage.

### Version 1.1
Hardening and stability improvements.

### Version 2.0
Discovery and relay.

### Version 2.1
File transfer and multi-device support.

### Version 3.0
Mesh routing and multi-hop delivery.

### Version 3.1+
Optimization, governance, monitoring, and AI hardening.

## Stakeholder Needs
- Users need confidentiality and control.
- Developers need clear requirements and architecture.
- Security reviewers need strong threat controls.
- Operations need runbooks, rollback, and observability.
- AI tooling needs bounded, documented access.

## Scope Boundaries
The system must keep a small trusted core. Features that expand the attack surface must be introduced only after the secure core passes testing and review.

## Success Metrics
- Secure messaging works end to end.
- Key handling is local and protected.
- Traceability is complete.
- Release gates are enforceable.
- Later phases build on stable prior phases.

## Scope Change Policy
Any change to scope must update requirements, RTM, risks, release plan, and relevant ADRs.
```


## 02-requirements.md

```markdown
# Software Requirements Specification

## 1. Purpose
This document defines the functional and non-functional requirements for PrivateMesh / النسر. Each requirement is intended to be specific, traceable, and testable.

## 2. Requirement Writing Rules
- Each requirement must have a unique ID.
- Each requirement must be verifiable.
- Each requirement must have a source.
- Each requirement must be linked to one or more use cases.
- Each requirement must be linked to design elements and test cases.
- Ambiguous language must be avoided.

## 3. Functional Requirements
### FR-001 Device Identity
The system shall generate a unique local identity for each device.

### FR-002 Key Generation
The system shall generate cryptographic keys locally on the device.

### FR-003 Secure Handshake
The system shall establish an authenticated secure session between peers.

### FR-004 Session Renewal
The system shall support session renewal and recovery after interruption.

### FR-005 Encrypted Messaging
The system shall encrypt messages end to end before transmission.

### FR-006 Message Integrity
The system shall verify message integrity and authenticity before delivery.

### FR-007 Local Storage Protection
The system shall store sensitive local data in encrypted form.

### FR-008 Peer Discovery
The system shall discover peers according to allowed policy.

### FR-009 Relay Transport
The system shall relay messages without exposing plaintext content.

### FR-010 File Transfer
The system shall support secure file transfer in chunks.

### FR-011 Multi-Device Support
The system shall allow one account or identity to be used across multiple authorized devices.

### FR-012 Mesh Routing
The system shall support multi-hop routing in later releases.

### FR-013 AI Assistance
The system shall provide limited AI-assisted support for documentation, classification, and anomaly hints.

## 4. Non-Functional Requirements
### NFR-001 Security
The system shall protect confidentiality, integrity, and authentication.

### NFR-002 Privacy
The system shall minimize metadata exposure.

### NFR-003 Performance
The system shall deliver acceptable message latency under expected load.

### NFR-004 Reliability
The system shall continue operating safely during partial failures.

### NFR-005 Recoverability
The system shall support rollback, restore, and recovery procedures.

### NFR-006 Maintainability
The system shall be modular, documented, and supportable.

### NFR-007 Testability
The system shall support unit, integration, security, fuzzing, and acceptance testing.

### NFR-008 Traceability
The system shall maintain bidirectional traceability across requirements, use cases, design, code, tests, and releases.

## 5. Security Requirements
### SR-001
Private keys shall not be stored in plaintext.

### SR-002
The system shall reject unauthenticated or tampered messages where authentication is required.

### SR-003
The system shall prevent replay attacks by using nonces or equivalent freshness controls.

### SR-004
The system shall reject downgrade attempts unless explicitly approved.

### SR-005
The system shall support key rotation and key revocation.

### SR-006
The system shall protect logs from leaking sensitive data.

### SR-007
The AI layer shall not control key management or make final security decisions.

## 6. Operational Requirements
### OR-001
The system shall provide deployment and rollback procedures.

### OR-002
The system shall provide monitoring and alerting.

### OR-003
The system shall provide restore procedures for backup data.

### OR-004
The system shall provide a documented incident response path.

## 7. Requirement Priorities
- Must: identity, handshake, sessions, encrypted messaging, local storage, security controls.
- Should: discovery, relay, file transfer, multi-device support.
- Could: AI-assisted analysis and documentation support.
- Won’t now: full mesh routing in the earliest release.

## 8. Verification
Each requirement must be verified by one or more test cases. Verification may include direct test, inspection, analysis, or demonstration.

## 9. Traceability
Each requirement must map to:
- source document,
- use case,
- architecture component,
- ADR,
- test case,
- release version.

## 10. Acceptance
A requirement is accepted for a release only if it is implemented, tested, documented, and linked in the traceability matrix.
```

# نعم، أكمل بقية المستندات والوثائق الفنية بنفس النسق والتنسيق

أكيد. سأكمل الآن **بقية المستندات والوثائق الفنية بنفس النسق**: حالات الاستخدام، المعمارية، نموذج التهديد، إدارة المفاتيح، مواصفات البروتوكول، الاكتشاف والترحيل، mesh، طبقة AI، خطة الإصدارات، الاختبارات، النشر، الحوكمة، ثم الملحقات النهائية [^87_1][^87_2][^87_3].\
سأحافظ على نفس أسلوب التوثيق: عنوان، هدف، بنود مرقمة، ونصوص قابلة للنسخ مباشرة إلى ملفات Markdown [^87_4][^87_5][^87_6].

## 03-use-cases.md

```markdown
# Use Cases

## UC-001: Create Device Identity
### Goal
Create a unique local identity for a new device.

### Actor
User or system.

### Preconditions
- Device is new or not yet initialized.
- Local secure storage is available.

### Main Flow
1. The system generates a key pair locally.
2. The system stores the private key securely.
3. The system exposes the public identity for peer exchange.
4. The system confirms identity creation.

### Alternate Flows
- If storage is unavailable, identity creation fails safely.
- If key generation fails, the system retries or reports an error.

### Postconditions
- Device has a valid local identity.

## UC-002: Establish Secure Session
### Goal
Create an authenticated encrypted session between peers.

### Actor
Peer device.

### Preconditions
- Both peers have valid identities.

### Main Flow
1. Peers exchange identity data.
2. The system performs handshake validation.
3. Session keys are derived.
4. The session is marked active.

### Alternate Flows
- If authentication fails, the session is rejected.
- If version negotiation fails, the session is aborted.

### Postconditions
- A secure session is ready for encrypted communication.

## UC-003: Send Message
### Goal
Transmit a message securely to another peer.

### Actor
User.

### Preconditions
- An active secure session exists.

### Main Flow
1. The user composes a message.
2. The system encrypts and signs it.
3. The system sends it to the recipient.
4. Delivery is acknowledged or queued for retry.

### Alternate Flows
- If transport fails, the message is retried according to policy.

### Postconditions
- The recipient receives a protected message or the system records failure.

## UC-004: Receive Message
### Goal
Receive and verify an incoming message.

### Actor
System.

### Preconditions
- An encrypted message arrives.

### Main Flow
1. The system validates the sender and session.
2. The system verifies integrity and freshness.
3. The system decrypts the payload.
4. The system delivers the message to the user or storage.

### Alternate Flows
- If integrity fails, the message is rejected.
- If replay is detected, the message is discarded.

### Postconditions
- Valid messages are delivered safely.

## UC-005: Discover Peer
### Goal
Find reachable peers under allowed policy.

### Actor
System.

### Preconditions
- Discovery is enabled.

### Main Flow
1. The system scans allowed discovery channels.
2. The system identifies candidate peers.
3. The system validates candidates against policy.
4. The system returns reachable peers.

### Postconditions
- The peer list is updated.

## UC-006: Relay Message
### Goal
Deliver a message through a relay without exposing plaintext.

### Actor
System.

### Preconditions
- Direct connection is unavailable.
- A permitted relay exists.

### Main Flow
1. The system wraps the encrypted message.
2. The system sends it through the relay.
3. The relay forwards without reading content.
4. The recipient confirms delivery.

### Alternate Flows
- If relay fails, another relay is selected or retry is attempted.

### Postconditions
- Message is delivered or queued safely.

## UC-007: Transfer File
### Goal
Send a file securely in chunks.

### Actor
User.

### Preconditions
- Secure session is active.
- File transfer is allowed.

### Main Flow
1. The file is chunked.
2. Each chunk is encrypted and tagged.
3. Chunks are transferred.
4. The recipient reassembles and verifies the file.

### Postconditions
- File arrives with integrity preserved.

## UC-008: Join Mesh Network
### Goal
Enable multi-hop routing between nodes.

### Actor
System.

### Preconditions
- Mesh is supported in the release.
- Trusted routing policy is configured.

### Main Flow
1. The system discovers mesh peers.
2. The system validates routing participation.
3. The system builds or updates routes.
4. The system begins forwarding approved traffic.

### Postconditions
- The device participates in mesh routing.
```


## 04-architecture.md

```markdown
# Architecture

## 1. Overview
The system is organized into layers that separate identity, sessions, messaging, transport, routing, storage, and AI assistance.

## 2. Architectural Goals
- Keep the trust boundary small.
- Separate cryptography from transport.
- Keep message content opaque to relays.
- Make the system testable and maintainable.
- Support staged release growth.

## 3. Layers
### 3.1 Identity Layer
Generates and manages device identity.

### 3.2 Key Management Layer
Handles key generation, rotation, storage, and revocation.

### 3.3 Session Layer
Creates, renews, and tears down secure sessions.

### 3.4 Message Layer
Encodes, encrypts, signs, and validates messages.

### 3.5 Transport Layer
Handles direct delivery and retry behavior.

### 3.6 Relay Layer
Forwards messages when direct delivery is not available.

### 3.7 Mesh Routing Layer
Supports multi-hop delivery in later releases.

### 3.8 Secure Storage Layer
Stores sensitive local data encrypted at rest.

### 3.9 AI Assistance Layer
Provides limited, policy-bound assistance for documentation and anomaly hints.

## 4. Main Components
- Identity Manager
- Key Manager
- Session Manager
- Message Codec
- Crypto Engine
- Discovery Service
- Relay Adapter
- Routing Engine
- Secure Store
- AI Assistant

## 5. Trust Boundaries
- Device boundary.
- Network boundary.
- Relay boundary.
- Mesh boundary.
- AI boundary.

## 6. Data Flow
1. Identity is created locally.
2. A session is established with a peer.
3. Messages are encrypted and signed.
4. Transport or relay carries the message.
5. The recipient verifies and decrypts it.
6. Data is stored securely when needed.

## 7. Design Principles
- Least privilege.
- Defense in depth.
- Local control of secrets.
- Explicit versioning.
- Clear failure modes.
- Traceable decisions.

## 8. ADR Policy
Any architecture decision that changes trust, flow, protocol behavior, or storage behavior must be written as an ADR.

## 9. Deployment View
The system should support development, test, staging, and production environments with distinct secrets and release controls.
```


## 05-threat-model.md

```markdown
# Threat Model

## 1. Purpose
This document identifies the system assets, attacker types, threats, and mitigations.

## 2. Assets
- Private keys.
- Session keys.
- Messages.
- Files.
- Metadata.
- Logs.
- Update packages.
- Trust configuration.

## 3. Attackers
- Network attacker.
- Relay attacker.
- Device attacker.
- Insider attacker.
- Downgrade attacker.
- Replay attacker.
- AI misuse scenario.

## 4. Threats
### 4.1 Interception
An attacker observes traffic in transit.

### 4.2 Impersonation
An attacker pretends to be a legitimate peer.

### 4.3 Replay
An attacker resends old messages or handshakes.

### 4.4 Tampering
An attacker modifies messages or metadata.

### 4.5 Key Compromise
An attacker gains access to private keys.

### 4.6 Downgrade
An attacker forces weaker behavior or older protocols.

### 4.7 Metadata Leakage
An attacker infers relationships or behavior from traffic patterns.

### 4.8 Malicious Relay
A relay attempts traffic analysis or abuse.

## 5. Mitigations
- End-to-end encryption.
- Mutual authentication.
- Nonces and freshness checks.
- Session binding.
- Key rotation and revocation.
- Protocol version validation.
- Logging controls.
- Minimal metadata design.
- Relay trust minimization.

## 6. Residual Risk
All remaining risk must be documented and accepted explicitly before release.

## 7. Review Policy
The threat model must be reviewed:
- During initial design.
- Before major release.
- After any significant architecture change.
- After any security incident.
```


## 06-security-key-management.md

```markdown
# Security and Key Management

## 1. Purpose
Define how keys are created, stored, used, rotated, revoked, backed up, and destroyed.

## 2. Rules
- Generate keys locally.
- Never store private keys in plaintext.
- Never log secret material.
- Protect backups.
- Revoke compromised keys quickly.
- Keep access tightly controlled.

## 3. Key Lifecycle
### 3.1 Generation
Generate key pairs locally during device initialization.

### 3.2 Storage
Store private keys only in protected local storage or equivalent secure vault.

### 3.3 Use
Use keys only for approved cryptographic operations.

### 3.4 Rotation
Rotate keys on schedule or after a security event.

### 3.5 Revocation
Revoke keys when compromise is suspected or confirmed.

### 3.6 Backup
Backups must be encrypted and access-controlled.

### 3.7 Recovery
Recovery must be documented and tested.

### 3.8 Destruction
Destroy retired secret material securely.

## 4. Security Controls
- Access control.
- Encryption at rest.
- Audit logging.
- Separation of duties.
- Recovery validation.
- Secure export policy.

## 5. Responsibilities
- Key Owner.
- Security Reviewer.
- Operations Owner.
- Recovery Approver.

## 6. Prohibited Practices
- Plaintext storage.
- Sharing private keys.
- Uncontrolled export.
- Unapproved recovery paths.
- AI-based key handling.
```


## 07-protocol-spec.md

```markdown
# Protocol Specification

## 1. Purpose
Define the message and session rules that govern communication.

## 2. Message Fields
- Version.
- Type.
- Sender ID.
- Receiver ID.
- Session ID.
- Timestamp.
- Nonce.
- Payload.
- Signature.
- Integrity field.

## 3. Session States
- Init.
- Hello.
- Challenge.
- Verified.
- Encrypted.
- Ack.
- Retry.
- Error.
- Revoked.
- Rekey.

## 4. Protocol Rules
- Reject replayed messages.
- Reject unauthenticated messages where required.
- Reject downgrade attempts.
- Validate version compatibility.
- Validate freshness.
- Validate integrity.

## 5. Compatibility
Protocol changes must preserve explicit version handling. Incompatible changes require a new version and updated ADRs.

## 6. Error Handling
- Invalid signature.
- Expired session.
- Unsupported version.
- Integrity failure.
- Replay detected.
- Policy denied.

## 7. Security Requirements
The protocol must not expose plaintext over transport, must bind messages to sessions, and must support safe rekeying.
```


## 08-discovery-relay.md

```markdown
# Discovery and Relay

## 1. Purpose
Define how peers are found and how messages are forwarded when direct delivery is not possible.

## 2. Discovery
- Local discovery.
- Policy-based discovery.
- Candidate validation.
- Reachability confirmation.

## 3. Relay
- Relay selection.
- Relay forwarding.
- Relay retry.
- Relay fallback.

## 4. Rules
- Relays must not see plaintext content.
- Discovery must follow policy.
- Retry must be bounded.
- Failure must be logged.

## 5. Fallback Modes
- Direct transport.
- Relay transport.
- Local-only mode.

## 6. Security Considerations
Relay paths must minimize trust, minimize metadata, and preserve confidentiality.
```


## 09-mesh-routing.md

```markdown
# Mesh Routing

## 1. Purpose
Define multi-hop routing for later releases.

## 2. Concepts
- Node.
- Link.
- Path.
- Hop.
- Route repair.
- Route validation.

## 3. Routing Rules
- Avoid loops.
- Prefer valid routes.
- Repair broken paths.
- Minimize unnecessary hops.
- Preserve content confidentiality.

## 4. Mesh Constraints
Mesh routing must not weaken the secure core. It must be introduced only after the base messaging and relay layers are stable.

## 5. Operational Notes
- Route updates must be observable.
- Failing nodes must be handled gracefully.
- Routing decisions should be auditable.
```


## 10-ai-layer.md

```markdown
# AI Layer

## 1. Purpose
Define the limited role of AI in the system.

## 2. Allowed Functions
- Summarize logs or documentation.
- Classify non-sensitive events.
- Suggest anomalies.
- Assist with documentation work.

## 3. Prohibited Functions
- Manage private keys.
- Make final security decisions.
- Expose sensitive content.
- Bypass access controls.
- Override human approval.

## 4. Safety Rules
- Use redaction where needed.
- Prefer local processing.
- Keep access limited.
- Log AI actions.
- Require explicit approval for sensitive actions.

## 5. Scope
AI is an assistant, not a controller.
```


## 11-release-plan.md

```markdown
# Release Plan

## 1. Release Philosophy
Release in small, controlled steps with explicit gates.

## 2. Versions
- 0.1 Proof of concept.
- 1.0 Secure core.
- 1.1 Stability hardening.
- 2.0 Discovery and relay.
- 2.1 Files and multi-device.
- 3.0 Mesh routing.
- 3.1+ Optimization and governance.

## 3. Release Gates
- Requirements completed.
- Tests passed.
- Risks reviewed.
- Rollback ready.
- Approvals obtained.

## 4. Rollout Strategy
- Internal alpha.
- Private beta.
- Staged rollout.
- Full release only after confidence is achieved.

## 5. Rollback Strategy
Each release must have a tested rollback plan before it is promoted.
```


## 12-testing-quality.md

```markdown
# Testing and Quality

## 1. Test Types
- Unit tests.
- Integration tests.
- Security tests.
- Fuzzing.
- Performance tests.
- Compatibility tests.
- Acceptance tests.

## 2. Quality Gates
- No critical open defects.
- No critical security failures.
- Traceability complete.
- Release checklist complete.

## 3. Coverage
Security-critical flows must always be covered by regression tests.

## 4. Evidence
Test results must be recorded and linked in the traceability matrix.
```


## 13-operations-runbook.md

```markdown
# Operations Runbook

## 1. Purpose
Provide repeatable operational procedures for deployment, monitoring, recovery, and incident handling.

## 2. Common Procedures
- Start service.
- Stop service.
- Deploy update.
- Roll back.
- Restore backup.
- Rotate keys.
- Handle incidents.
- Review logs.

## 3. Monitoring
- Health status.
- Delivery success.
- Authentication failures.
- Relay anomalies.
- Resource usage.
- Storage errors.

## 4. Incident Handling
1. Contain.
2. Preserve evidence.
3. Notify owner.
4. Mitigate.
5. Recover.
6. Document.

## 5. Logging
Avoid logging sensitive data. Protect logs and retain them per policy.

## 6. Contacts
- Release Owner.
- Security Lead.
- Operations Lead.
- Documentation Owner.
```


## 14-governance-change.md

```markdown
# Governance and Change Control

## 1. Purpose
Define who approves changes and how the project stays controlled.

## 2. Roles
- Project Owner.
- Technical Lead.
- Security Reviewer.
- Operations Owner.
- Documentation Owner.
- AI Safety Reviewer.

## 3. Change Policy
- Significant architectural changes require an ADR.
- Requirement changes require RTM updates.
- Release changes require checklist updates.
- Risk changes require risk register updates.

## 4. Approval Rules
No critical change may be implemented without review and approval.

## 5. Documentation Policy
Documentation must be versioned, dated, and kept consistent with implementation.
```

## 18-release-checklist.md

```markdown
# Release Checklist

## Pre-Release Checks
- [ ] All documents are updated.
- [ ] RTM is current.
- [ ] Risk register is reviewed.
- [ ] Required ADRs are approved.
- [ ] Test suite passed.
- [ ] Security tests passed.
- [ ] Regression tests passed.
- [ ] Performance checks passed.
- [ ] Rollback plan exists and is verified.
- [ ] Backup and restore procedures are validated.
- [ ] Release notes are written.
- [ ] Monitoring is ready.
- [ ] Operational contacts are confirmed.
- [ ] Sign-off has been obtained.

## Go Criteria
- No critical defects.
- No critical security findings.
- Rollback is ready.
- Required approvals are complete.

## No-Go Criteria
- Critical defect remains open.
- Security issue is unresolved.
- Evidence is missing.
- Rollback is not tested.

## Post-Release Checks
- [ ] Service stable.
- [ ] Error rate within limits.
- [ ] Logs and telemetry accessible.
- [ ] Rollback remains available.
```


## 15-adr/ADR-0001.md

```markdown
# ADR-0001: Generate device identity locally

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
The system needs a trustworthy identity model without relying on a central identity authority.

## Decision
Device identity will be generated locally on the device, and the private key will remain under local protected storage.

## Alternatives
1. Central identity server.
2. Cloud identity service.
3. Local identity generation.

## Rationale
Local generation preserves decentralization, reduces trust in external infrastructure, and keeps private keys under device control.

## Consequences
### Positive
- Better privacy.
- Lower central dependency.
- Smaller trust boundary.

### Negative
- Recovery and revocation must be designed carefully.
- Device loss has greater impact.
```


## 15-adr/ADR-0002.md

```markdown
# ADR-0002: Use authenticated session keys for messaging

## Status
Accepted

## Date
2026-09-28

## Context
Messages require confidentiality and integrity without exposing long-term identity keys for every message.

## Decision
The system will establish authenticated session keys during handshake and use them for message encryption and integrity protection.

## Alternatives
1. Encrypt every message with the identity key.
2. Use unauthenticated session encryption.
3. Use authenticated session keys.

## Rationale
Session keys reduce exposure of long-term keys and support renewal and revocation.

## Consequences
- More handshake complexity.
- Better security separation between identity and transport.
```


## appendices/glossary.md

```markdown
# Glossary

- **Identity**: The device’s cryptographic identifier.
- **Session**: A temporary secure communication context.
- **Relay**: A forwarding node that does not inspect plaintext.
- **Mesh**: A multi-hop peer network.
- **Nonce**: A freshness value used to prevent replay.
- **ADR**: Architecture Decision Record.
- **RTM**: Requirements Traceability Matrix.
- **Runbook**: A documented operational procedure.
```

```markdown
- AI: Artificial Intelligence
- ADR: Architecture Decision Record
- E2EE: End-to-End Encryption
- KMS: Key Management System
- RTM: Requirements Traceability Matrix
- QA: Quality Assurance
- DRI: Directly Responsible Individual
- PoC: Proof of Concept
```

# اكتب وثيقة المعمارية ونموذج التهديد تفصيلياً الآن

أكيد. فيما يلي **وثيقة المعمارية** و**نموذج التهديد** بصيغة تفصيلية ومكتملة، مع تنظيم مناسب ليُحفظ مباشرة كملفين Markdown داخل المستودع [^88_1][^88_2][^88_3].\
اعتمدت هنا على منهجية واضحة: تحديد النطاق، تفكيك النظام، توثيق التدفقات والثقة، ثم ربط التهديدات بالضوابط والأدلة، لأن هذا هو الشكل الأكثر فاعلية في الوثائق الأمنية والمعمارية [^88_4][^88_5][^88_6].

# 04-architecture.md

```markdown
# Architecture

## 1. Purpose
This document describes the architecture of PrivateMesh / النسر. It explains the system structure, the major components, the trust boundaries, the data flows, the design principles, and the architectural decisions that shape the product.

## 2. Scope
This architecture covers the secure communication core, including device identity, key management, sessions, encrypted messaging, local secure storage, discovery, relay transport, and future mesh routing. It also defines the constrained AI support layer and the operational boundaries around it.

## 3. Architectural Goals
- Preserve device-local control of secrets.
- Keep the trusted computing base as small as possible.
- Protect message content end to end.
- Minimize metadata exposure.
- Support staged release growth.
- Keep the system observable, testable, and maintainable.
- Separate security-critical logic from non-critical support logic.

## 4. Design Principles
- Security first.
- Privacy first.
- Local-first secret handling.
- Least privilege.
- Defense in depth.
- Explicit versioning.
- Fail closed where appropriate.
- Avoid hidden centralization.
- Record major decisions in ADRs.

## 5. System Context
The system exists in a distributed environment where devices communicate directly when possible and use permitted relay or routing services when direct delivery is unavailable. No central authority should be required to interpret message contents. Trust should remain local, explicit, and limited.

## 6. High-Level View
The architecture is organized into the following layers:

### 6.1 Identity Layer
Responsible for local device identity generation and peer identity presentation.

### 6.2 Key Management Layer
Responsible for generating, storing, rotating, revoking, and recovering cryptographic keys.

### 6.3 Session Layer
Responsible for secure handshakes, authentication, session establishment, session renewal, and session termination.

### 6.4 Message Layer
Responsible for message formatting, encryption, signing, integrity validation, and replay protection.

### 6.5 Transport Layer
Responsible for delivery over direct or indirect network paths.

### 6.6 Relay Layer
Responsible for forwarding encrypted payloads when direct transport is unavailable.

### 6.7 Discovery Layer
Responsible for peer discovery and reachability checks within policy.

### 6.8 Mesh Routing Layer
Responsible for multi-hop forwarding and route repair in later releases.

### 6.9 Storage Layer
Responsible for secure local persistence of sensitive state and queued messages.

### 6.10 AI Assistance Layer
Responsible for bounded documentation assistance and anomaly suggestions, never for authoritative security operations.

## 7. Major Components
### 7.1 Identity Manager
Creates and manages the device identity. It exposes public identity material and coordinates with the key manager.

### 7.2 Key Manager
Handles all cryptographic key lifecycle operations. It is the only component allowed to manage secret material.

### 7.3 Session Manager
Manages handshakes, authenticated session creation, session renewal, and expiration.

### 7.4 Message Codec
Encodes message structures, applies encryption, attaches integrity data, and validates incoming payloads.

### 7.5 Discovery Service
Discovers peers according to local policy and network reachability.

### 7.6 Relay Adapter
Delivers encrypted traffic through allowed relays without exposing plaintext to the relay.

### 7.7 Routing Engine
Supports future mesh behavior, route selection, and path repair.

### 7.8 Secure Store
Persists local data, metadata needed for operation, and queued messages in protected form.

### 7.9 AI Assistant Layer
Provides limited summarization, classification, and anomaly hints for non-sensitive operational or documentation tasks.

### 7.10 Observability Subsystem
Collects logs, metrics, and traces while excluding sensitive content.

## 8. Trust Boundaries
### 8.1 Device Boundary
The local device is the primary boundary. Secret operations should occur only within this boundary.

### 8.2 Network Boundary
The network is untrusted. Traffic across the network must be protected against interception and tampering.

### 8.3 Relay Boundary
Relay nodes are not trusted with plaintext. They may forward encrypted data only.

### 8.4 Mesh Boundary
Mesh nodes may participate in forwarding but are not trusted with message content.

### 8.5 AI Boundary
AI components are support tools only. They must not handle private keys or make final security decisions.

## 9. Data Flows
### 9.1 Identity Creation Flow
1. The device initializes locally.
2. The key manager generates a key pair.
3. The private key is stored securely.
4. The public identity is published for peer exchange.

### 9.2 Handshake Flow
1. Peers exchange identity material.
2. The session manager validates version compatibility and trust assumptions.
3. Session keys are derived.
4. A secure session is activated.

### 9.3 Message Flow
1. The sender creates a message.
2. The message codec encrypts and signs the payload.
3. The transport layer sends it directly or via relay.
4. The recipient validates, decrypts, and delivers it.

### 9.4 Relay Flow
1. Direct delivery is unavailable.
2. The relay adapter wraps the encrypted payload.
3. The relay forwards without inspecting plaintext.
4. The recipient receives and validates the message.

### 9.5 Storage Flow
1. Sensitive data is persisted only when required.
2. The secure store encrypts data at rest.
3. Access is controlled by local policy and session state.

### 9.6 Recovery Flow
1. A failure or compromise is detected.
2. The system revokes or rotates affected keys.
3. The system restores safe state from approved backups if needed.
4. The system re-establishes trust through the session layer.

## 10. Security Properties
- Confidentiality of message content.
- Integrity of messages and sessions.
- Authentication of peers.
- Freshness against replay.
- Local control of secrets.
- Reduced trust in network intermediaries.
- Explicit handling of downgrade resistance.
- Auditable architecture decisions.

## 11. Non-Security Quality Attributes
### 11.1 Reliability
The system should remain usable under partial failure, network disruption, and relay unavailability.

### 11.2 Maintainability
The architecture must remain modular and document-driven.

### 11.3 Testability
Each major layer must be independently testable.

### 11.4 Performance
The design should avoid unnecessary overhead in message delivery and session renewal.

### 11.5 Scalability
The system should scale in capability by adding layers and routing strategies without breaking the secure core.

## 12. Architectural Decisions
Major decisions must be captured in ADRs, including but not limited to:
- Local identity generation.
- Protected key storage.
- Authenticated session keys.
- Protocol downgrade rejection.
- Deferred mesh routing.
- Limited AI role.
- Relay confidentiality rules.

## 13. Deployment View
### 13.1 Environments
- Development.
- Test.
- Staging.
- Production.

### 13.2 Separation
Each environment must use separate secrets, separate policies, and separate release controls.

### 13.3 Release Gating
A release may not advance without successful verification, documented traceability, and approved rollback.

## 14. Logging and Observability
Logs must support debugging and incident response without revealing sensitive payloads or key material. Sensitive metadata should be minimized and redacted when possible.

## 15. Open Issues
- Final algorithm selection for some protocol fields.
- Final mesh routing strategy.
- Final device recovery model for multi-device use.
- AI scope expansion controls.

## 16. Summary
This architecture intentionally keeps the secure core small and explicit. The rest of the system grows around that core through documented and reviewable decisions.
```

# 05-threat-model.md

```markdown
# Threat Model

## 1. Purpose
This document records what the system protects, who may attack it, what can go wrong, what controls exist, and what remains risky. It is intended to be a living document that changes as the architecture and release scope change.

## 2. Scope
This threat model covers:
- Device identity.
- Key management.
- Secure sessions.
- Message transport.
- Relay usage.
- Secure storage.
- File transfer.
- Multi-device support.
- Future mesh routing.
- Limited AI assistance.
- Release and operations tooling.

## 3. Modeling Approach
The model uses a structured decomposition approach:
1. Define scope.
2. Map architecture and trust boundaries.
3. Identify assets and security goals.
4. Identify threats and misuse cases.
5. Rate risk.
6. Define mitigations.
7. Record decisions and evidence.
8. Review and update regularly.

## 4. Security Objectives
- Protect confidentiality.
- Protect integrity.
- Authenticate peers.
- Prevent replay.
- Prevent downgrade attacks.
- Reduce metadata leakage.
- Preserve availability.
- Control secret material.
- Restrict AI to non-authoritative support tasks.

## 5. Assets
### 5.1 Critical Assets
- Private keys.
- Session keys.
- Identity material.
- Message contents.
- File contents.
- Trust configuration.
- Recovery material.
- Update artifacts.
- Security logs and evidence.

### 5.2 Sensitive Supporting Assets
- Metadata.
- Peer lists.
- Discovery information.
- Route data.
- Operational alerts.
- Audit records.

## 6. Trust Boundaries
### 6.1 Device Boundary
The device boundary is trusted only for approved local operations. Compromised devices must be treated as hostile.

### 6.2 Network Boundary
The network is untrusted. Any data in transit must be assumed observable or modifiable.

### 6.3 Relay Boundary
Relay nodes must be treated as untrusted for content. They may only forward encrypted material.

### 6.4 Mesh Boundary
Mesh participants must not be trusted with plaintext. Routing information should be minimized where possible.

### 6.5 AI Boundary
AI assistance is untrusted for security decisions. It may assist with summaries or anomaly hints only.

## 7. Actors
- Legitimate user.
- Legitimate peer device.
- Relay operator.
- Network attacker.
- Compromised peer.
- Insider with limited access.
- Malicious update source.
- Misconfigured operator.
- Misused AI assistant.

## 8. Threat Categories
### 8.1 Interception
An attacker captures traffic in transit to learn content or metadata.

### 8.2 Tampering
An attacker modifies messages, session data, or update artifacts.

### 8.3 Spoofing
An attacker impersonates a device or peer.

### 8.4 Replay
An attacker resends old messages, handshakes, or commands.

### 8.5 Downgrade
An attacker forces weaker protocol behavior or legacy compatibility.

### 8.6 Privilege Abuse
A component or operator uses access beyond its permitted scope.

### 8.7 Data Leakage
Sensitive content or metadata leaks through logs, AI prompts, cache, or storage.

### 8.8 Availability Attack
An attacker disrupts service, overloads relay paths, or blocks delivery.

### 8.9 Recovery Failure
A compromise or fault cannot be recovered safely because backup, revocation, or rollback is incomplete.

## 9. Detailed Threat Scenarios
### 9.1 Key Theft
If private keys are exposed from local storage, an attacker can impersonate the device and decrypt traffic.

**Impact:** Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Protected storage, encryption at rest, limited access, rotation, revocation, secure backup policy, incident response.

### 9.2 Replay of Session Messages
An attacker may resend previously captured packets to confuse session state or re-trigger actions.

**Impact:** High to Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Nonces, timestamps, sequence checks, session binding, freshness validation, expiry rules.

### 9.3 Man-in-the-Middle on Handshake
An attacker intercepts the handshake and attempts to establish separate sessions or inject false identities.

**Impact:** Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Mutual authentication, identity validation, signed handshake elements, downgrade rejection.

### 9.4 Relay Metadata Analysis
A relay may inspect timing, size, or routing patterns to infer relationships or user behavior.

**Impact:** High.  
**Mitigations:** Minimize metadata, batch or pad where feasible, limit relay trust, avoid plaintext at relay, policy controls.

### 9.5 Tampered Update Package
A malicious update could alter system behavior or insert backdoors.

**Impact:** Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Signed releases, verification before install, controlled rollout, rollback, release checklist, integrity checks.

### 9.6 AI Leakage
A prompt or summary generated by AI may expose sensitive content or keys.

**Impact:** High to Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Redaction, scope restrictions, local processing preference, prompt filtering, no key access, audit logging.

### 9.7 Downgrade to Weak Protocol
A peer or attacker tries to force an older or weaker protocol version.

**Impact:** Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Explicit version negotiation, default reject behavior, security policy gating, compatibility exceptions only by review.

### 9.8 Storage Corruption
Local data corruption causes message loss, incorrect state, or invalid recovery.

**Impact:** High.  
**Mitigations:** Integrity checks, journaling, tested restore, safe error handling, backup validation.

### 9.9 Relay Abuse or Flooding
A malicious actor overloads relay or discovery services, affecting availability.

**Impact:** Medium to High.  
**Mitigations:** Rate limiting, quotas, abuse detection, health checks, relay selection policy.

### 9.10 Multi-Device Trust Confusion
A new device may be added without proper authorization, causing account compromise.

**Impact:** Critical.  
**Mitigations:** Explicit enrollment, approval workflow, revocation support, device inventory, audit trail.

## 10. Misuse Cases
- A user shares a private key outside policy.
- A relay logs content it should not retain.
- AI is asked to summarize secret data.
- An operator bypasses approval for a risky release.
- A compromised device continues to be treated as trusted.
- A deprecated protocol path is left enabled.

## 11. Risk Rating Method
Risk should be rated using at least:
- Likelihood.
- Impact.
- Exposure.
- Exploitability.
- Security effect.
- Privacy effect.
- Operational burden.

## 12. Mitigation Strategy
Each threat must map to one or more of the following:
- Design change.
- Code control.
- Policy control.
- Operational procedure.
- Test case.
- Monitoring alert.
- Release gate.
- Human approval.

## 13. Verification
Every major mitigation should have evidence, such as:
- Security tests.
- Fuzzing.
- Code review.
- Static analysis.
- Manual review.
- Incident drill.
- Restore test.
- Release checklist entry.

## 14. Residual Risk
Some risks cannot be eliminated completely. Residual risk must be explicitly accepted by the responsible owner, with a review date.

## 15. Review Triggers
The threat model must be updated when:
- Architecture changes.
- Protocol changes.
- Key handling changes.
- A new release stage begins.
- A security incident occurs.
- AI scope changes.
- A new trust boundary is added.

## 16. Summary
This threat model treats secrets, content, metadata, and trust boundaries as first-class concerns. The goal is not only to identify attacks, but to make mitigations testable, reviewable, and maintainable.
```

إذا أردت، أكتب الآن مباشرة **وثيقة البروتوكول** و**وثيقة إدارة المفاتيح** و**وثيقة الاكتشاف والترحيل** بنفس هذا التفصيل.

# تابع

أكيد. فيما يلي **الوثائق الثلاثة التفصيلية التالية**: **إدارة المفاتيح، مواصفات البروتوكول، والاكتشاف والترحيل**، مكتوبة بصيغة تصلح مباشرة كملفات Markdown داخل المستودع [^89_1][^89_2][^89_3].\
اعتمدت فيها على أفضل الممارسات الخاصة بإدارة دورة حياة المفاتيح، توثيق الرسائل والحالات، وربط الاكتشاف والـ relay بالسياسات والأمان [^89_4][^89_5][^89_6].
